Each Apply function takes an \\\"apply\\n25: configuration\\\" type as an argument, which is a structured representation of an Apply request. For\\n26: example:\\n27: \\n28: \\timport (\\n29: \\t     ...\\n30: \\t     v1ac \\\"k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/autoscaling/v1\\\"\\n31: \\t)\\n32: \\thpaApplyConfig := v1ac.HorizontalPodAutoscaler(autoscalerName, ns).\\n33: \\t     WithSpec(v1ac.HorizontalPodAutoscalerSpec().\\n34: \\t              WithMinReplicas(0)\\n35: \\t     )\\n36: \\treturn hpav1client.Apply(ctx, hpaApplyConfig, metav1.ApplyOptions{FieldManager: \\\"mycontroller\\\", Force: true})\\n37: \\n38: Note in this example that HorizontalPodAutoscaler is imported from an \\\"applyconfigurations\\\"\\n39: package. Each \\\"apply configuration\\\" type represents the same Kubernetes object kind as the\\n40: corresponding go struct, but where all fields are pointers to make them optional, allowing apply\\n41: requests to be accurately represented. For example, this when the apply configuration in the above\\n42: example is marshalled to YAML, it produces:\\n43: \\n44: \\tapiVersion: autoscaling/v1\\n45: \\tkind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler\\n46: \\tmetadata:\\n47: \\t    name: myHPA\\n48: \\t    namespace: myNamespace\\n49: \\tspec:\\n50: \\t    minReplicas: 0\\n51: \\n52: To understand why this is needed, the above YAML cannot be produced by the\\n53: v1.HorizontalPodAutoscaler go struct. Take for example:\\n54: \\n55: \\thpa := v1.HorizontalPodAutoscaler{\\n56: \\t     TypeMeta: metav1.TypeMeta{\\n57: \\t              APIVersion: \\\"autoscaling/v1\\\",\\n58: \\t              Kind:       \\\"HorizontalPodAutoscaler\\\",\\n59: \\t     },\\n60: \\t     ObjectMeta: ObjectMeta{\\n61: \\t              Namespace: ns,\\n62: \\t              Name:      autoscalerName,\\n63: \\t     },\\n64: \\t     Spec: v1.HorizontalPodAutoscalerSpec{\\n65: \\t              MinReplicas: pointer.Int32Ptr(0),\\n66: \\t     },\\n67: \\t}\\n68: \\n69: The above code attempts to declare the same apply configuration as shown in the previous examples,\\n70: but when marshalled to YAML, produces:\\n71: \\n72: \\tkind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler\\n73: \\tapiVersion: autoscaling/v1\\n74: \\tmetadata:\\n75: \\t  name: myHPA\\n76: \\t  namespace: myNamespace\\n77: \\tspec:\\n78: \\t  scaleTargetRef:\\n79: \\t    kind: \\\"\\\"\\n80: \\t    name: \\\"\\\"\\n81: \\t  minReplicas: 0\\n82: \\t  maxReplicas: 0\\n83: \\n84: Which, among other things, contains spec.maxReplicas set to 0. This is almost certainly not what\\n85: the caller intended (the intended apply configuration says nothing about the maxReplicas field),\\n86: and could have serious consequences on a production system: it directs the autoscaler to downscale\\n87: to zero pods. The problem here originates from the fact that the go structs contain required fields\\n88: that are zero valued if not set explicitly. The go structs work as intended for create and update\\n89: operations, but are fundamentally incompatible with apply, which is why we have introduced the\\n90: generated \\\"apply configuration\\\" types.\\n91: \\n92: The \\\"apply configurations\\\" also have convenience With<FieldName> functions that make it easier to\\n93: build apply requests. This allows developers to set fields without having to deal with the fact that\\n94: all the fields in the \\\"apply configuration\\\" types are pointers, and are inconvenient to set using\\n95: go. For example \\\"MinReplicas: &0\\\" is not legal go code, so without the With functions, developers\\n96: would work around this problem by using a library, .e.g. \\\"MinReplicas: pointer.Int32Ptr(0)\\\", but\\n97: string enumerations like corev1.Protocol are still a problem since they cannot be supported by a\\n98: general purpose library. In addition to the convenience, the With functions also isolate\\n99: developers from the underlying representation, which makes it safer for the underlying\\n100: representation to be changed to support additional features in the future.\\n101: \\n102: # Controller Support\\n103: \\n104: The new client-go support makes it much easier to use Server-side Apply in controllers, by either of\\n105: two mechanisms.\\n106: \\n107: Mechanism 1:\\n108: \\n109: When authoring new controllers to use Server-side Apply, a good approach is to have the controller\\n110: recreate the apply configuration for an object each time it reconciles that object.  This ensures\\n111: that the controller fully reconciles all the fields that it is responsible for. Controllers\\n112: typically should unconditionally set all the fields they own by setting \\\"Force: true\\\" in the\\n113: ApplyOptions. Controllers must also provide a FieldManager name that is unique to the\\n114: reconciliation loop that apply is called from.\\n115: \\n116: When upgrading existing controllers to use Server-side Apply the same approach often works\\n117: well--migrate the controllers to recreate the apply configuration each time it reconciles any\\n118: object. For cases where this does not work well, see Mechanism 2.\\n119: \\n120: Mechanism 2:\\n121: \\n122: When upgrading existing controllers to use Server-side Apply, the controller might have multiple\\n123: code paths that update different parts of an object depending on various conditions. Migrating a\\n124: controller like this to Server-side Apply can be risky because if the controller forgets to include\\n125: any fields in an apply configuration that is included in a previous apply request, a field can be\\n126: accidentally deleted. For such cases, an alternative to mechanism 1 is to replace any controller\\n127: reconciliation code that performs a \\\"read/modify-in-place/update\\\" (or patch) workflow with a\\n128: \\\"extract/modify-in-place/apply\\\" workflow. Here's an example of the new workflow:\\n129: \\n130: \\t    fieldMgr := \\\"my-field-manager\\\"\\n131: \\t    deploymentClient := clientset.AppsV1().Deployments(\\\"default\\\")\\n132: \\t    // read, could also be read from a shared informer\\n133: \\t    deployment, err := deploymentClient.Get(ctx, \\\"example-deployment\\\", metav1.GetOptions{})\\n134: \\t    if err != nil {\\n135: \\t      // handle error\\n136: \\t    }\\n137: \\t    // extract\\n138: \\t    deploymentApplyConfig, err := appsv1ac.ExtractDeployment(deployment, fieldMgr)\\n139: \\t    if err != nil {\\n140: \\t      // handle error\\n141: \\t    }\\n142: \\t    // modify-in-place\\n143: \\t    deploymentApplyConfig.Spec.Template.Spec.WithContainers(corev1ac.Container().\\n144: \\t\\tWithName(\\\"modify-slice\\\").\\n145: \\t\\tWithImage(\\\"nginx:1.14.2\\\"),\\n146: \\t    )\\n147: \\t    // apply\\n148: \\t    applied, err := deploymentClient.Apply(ctx, Each \"apply configuration\" type represents the same Kubernetes object kind as the\n40: corresponding go struct, but where all fields are pointers to make them optional, allowing apply\n41: requests to be accurately represented. For example, this when the apply configuration in the above\n42: example is marshalled to YAML, it produces:\n43: \n44: \tapiVersion: autoscaling/v1\n45: \tkind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler\n46: \tmetadata:\n47: \t    name: myHPA\n48: \t    namespace: myNamespace\n49: \tspec:\n50: \t    minReplicas: 0\n51: \n52: To understand why this is needed, the above YAML cannot be produced by the\n53: v1.HorizontalPodAutoscaler go struct. Take for example:\n54: \n55: \thpa := v1.HorizontalPodAutoscaler{\n56: \t     TypeMeta: metav1.TypeMeta{\n57: \t              APIVersion: \"autoscaling/v1\",\n58: \t              Kind:       \"HorizontalPodAutoscaler\",\n59: \t     },\n60: \t     ObjectMeta: ObjectMeta{\n61: \t              Namespace: ns,\n62: \t              Name:      autoscalerName,\n63: \t     },\n64: \t     Spec: v1.HorizontalPodAutoscalerSpec{\n65: \t              MinReplicas: pointer.Int32Ptr(0),\n66: \t     },\n67: \t}\n68: \n69: The above code attempts to declare the same apply configuration as shown in the previous examples,\n70: but when marshalled to YAML, produces:\n71: \n72: \tkind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler\n73: \tapiVersion: autoscaling/v1\n74: \tmetadata:\n75: \t  name: myHPA\n76: \t  namespace: myNamespace\n77: \tspec:\n78: \t  scaleTargetRef:\n79: \t    kind: \"\"\n80: \t    name: \"\"\n81: \t  minReplicas: 0\n82: \t  maxReplicas: 0\n83: \n84: Which, among other things, contains spec.maxReplicas set to 0. This is almost certainly not what\n85: the caller intended (the intended apply configuration says nothing about the maxReplicas field),\n86: and could have serious consequences on a production system: it directs the autoscaler to downscale\n87: to zero pods. The problem here originates from the fact that the go structs contain required fields\n88: that are zero valued if not set explicitly. The go structs work as intended for create and update\n89: operations, but are fundamentally incompatible with apply, which is why we have introduced the\n90: generated \"apply configuration\" types.\n91: \n92: The \"apply configurations\" also have convenience With<FieldName> functions that make it easier to\n93: build apply requests. This allows developers to set fields without having to deal with the fact that\n94: all the fields in the \"apply configuration\" types are pointers, and are inconvenient to set using\n95: go. For example \"MinReplicas: &0\" is not legal go code, so without the With functions, developers\n96: would work around this problem by using a library, .e.g. \"MinReplicas: pointer.Int32Ptr(0)\", but\n97: string enumerations like corev1.Protocol are still a problem since they cannot be supported by a\n98: general purpose library. In addition to the convenience, the With functions also isolate\n99: developers from the underlying representation, which makes it safer for the underlying\n100: representation to be changed to support additional features in the future.\n101: \n102: # Controller Support\n103: \n104: The new client-go support makes it much easier to use Server-side Apply in controllers, by either of\n105: two mechanisms.\n106: \n107: Mechanism 1:\n108: \n109: When authoring new controllers to use Server-side Apply, a good approach is to have the controller\n110: recreate the apply configuration for an object each time it reconciles that object.  This ensures\n111: that the controller fully reconciles all the fields that it is responsible for. Controllers\n112: typically should unconditionally set all the fields they own by setting \"Force: true\" in the\n113: ApplyOptions. Controllers must also provide a FieldManager name that is unique to the\n114: reconciliation loop that apply is called from.\n115: \n116: When upgrading existing controllers to use Server-side Apply the same approach often works\n117: well--migrate the controllers to recreate the apply configuration each time it reconciles any\n118: object. For cases where this does not work well, see Mechanism 2.\n119: \n120: Mechanism 2:\n121: \n122: When upgrading existing controllers to use Server-side Apply, the controller might have multiple\n123: code paths that update different parts of an object depending on various conditions. Migrating a\n124: controller like this to Server-side Apply can be risky because if the controller forgets to include\n125: any fields in an apply configuration that is included in a previous apply request, a field can be\n126: accidentally deleted. For such cases, an alternative to mechanism 1 is to replace any controller\n127: reconciliation code that performs a \"read/modify-in-place/update\" (or patch) workflow with a\n128: \"extract/modify-in-place/apply\" workflow. Here's an example of the new workflow:\n129: \n130: \t    fieldMgr := \"my-field-manager\"\n131: \t    deploymentClient := clientset.AppsV1().Deployments(\"default\")\n132: \t    // read, could also be read from a shared informer\n133: \t    deployment, err := deploymentClient.Get(ctx, \"example-deployment\", metav1.GetOptions{})\n134: \t    if err != nil {\n135: \t      // handle error\n136: \t    }\n137: \t    // extract\n138: \t    deploymentApplyConfig, err := appsv1ac.ExtractDeployment(deployment, fieldMgr)\n139: \t    if err != nil {\n140: \t      // handle error\n141: \t    }\n142: \t    // modify-in-place\n143: \t    deploymentApplyConfig.Spec.Template.Spec.WithContainers(corev1ac.Container().\n144: \t\tWithName(\"modify-slice\").\n145: \t\tWithImage(\"nginx:1.14.2\"),\n146: \t    )\n147: \t    // apply\n148: \t    applied, err := deploymentClient.Apply(ctx, extractedDeployment, metav1.ApplyOptions{FieldManager: fieldMgr})\n149: */\n150: package applyconfigurations\n151: ","url":"https://sourcegraph.sourcegraph.com/github.com/kubernetes/kubernetes@b08fa0cdf18610be74d9741cf361446a5f42ff76/-/blob/staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/doc.go"}}}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_011bAVFEtTAfGcw7t3uHkYAE","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"I now have a comprehensive understanding of the Server-Side Apply subsystem. Let me now write the documentation.\n\nKey things I've learned:\n1. **applyconfigurations package** - Located in `staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/`. This is the client-side representation of apply requests - struct types with all fields as pointers to allow omitting unset fields.\n\n2. **applyconfiguration-gen tool** - Located in `staging/src/k8s.io/code-generator/cmd/applyconfiguration-gen/`. It generates the applyconfigurations from the type Go definitions + OpenAPI schema.\n\n\n3. **managedfields subsystem** - Located in `staging/src/k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/util/managedfields/`. This is the core of SSA - the `FieldManager` which wraps a layered stack of `Manager` implementations.\n\n4. **patch handler** - Located in `staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/endpoints/handlers/patch.go`. This is where the HTTP request enters the SSA path.\n\n5. **structured-merge-diff library** (`sigs.k8s.io/structured-merge-diff/v6`) - the external library that actually performs the merge\n\n6. **Extract workflow** - The `ExtractInto()` function in `extract.go` handles extracting field information from managed objects.\n\nNow I'm putting together the full architecture picture. Server-Side Apply lets clients declare which fields they want to manage, and the server handles the merge logic, conflict detection, and tracks ownership through `metadata.managedFields`. The main pieces are the client-side apply configuration types, the code generator that creates them, the server-side field manager and merge engine, the external merge library, and the HTTP handler that routes requests into the SSA flow.\n\nThe field manager itself is built as a stack of decorators wrapping the core merge logic. `VersionCheckManager` ensures the object has the right group-version-kind, `LastAppliedUpdater` keeps kubectl's annotation in sync, and then there are several other layers handling probabilistic skipping, manager info building, and metadata stripping before reaching the actual structured merge diff engine at the bottom.\n\nThe `TypeConverter` acts as the translation layer between Kubernetes runtime objects and the SMD library's type system.  It converts OpenAPI schemas to SMD format, builds GVK-indexed parsers, and transforms objects into typed values accordingly. For CRDs lacking schemas, `DeducedTypeConverter` treats all lists as atomic.\n\nWhen an SSA request comes in, the HTTP handler creates an `applyPatcher` with the FieldManager and initiates the patch flow through `GuaranteedUpdate()`.\n\nInside the update, `applyPatch()` either creates a new object if one doesn't exist or applies the patch to the current object via `fieldManager.Apply()`. The field manager decodes managed fields, runs the Manager stack's Apply logic, handles conflicts by converting them to HTTP 409 responses, and re-encodes the updated managed fields. The `structuredMergeManager` then validates the patch version, converts both patch and live objects to typed representations, and continues with the merge operation.\n\nWhen conflicts occur—meaning an applier tries to claim a field owned by another manager—the SMD library returns a conflicts list that gets converted to HTTP 409 responses with field manager conflict causes. Resolvers can force ownership, use different manager names to operate independently, or remove fields from the apply config to relinquish ownership.\n\nNow I'm looking at the extract workflow...\n\nGenerating apply configuration code... The `CapManagersManager` prevents unbounded growth by keeping only the 10 most recent update managers and merging older entries. There's also a `LastAppliedUpdater` that maintains backward compatibility with kubectl's client-side apply by syncing the last-applied-configuration annotation. CRDs handle merging differently through `NewCRDStructuredMergeManager`, which enforces atomic-only list semantics, and scale subresources get special treatment via the `ScaleHandler` to map field paths between the main resource and its scale view.\n\nOn first apply to a fresh object, the `ProbabilisticSkipNonAppliedManager` optimizes by registering existing fields under a bootstrap manager before executing the actual apply {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_011bAVFEtTAfGcw7t3uHkYAE","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_0175qtyHaMbgUtgMdeuYFkie","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/workspace/documentation.md","content":"# Kubernetes Server-Side Apply and applyconfigurations: A Deep Guide\n\n## 1. Subsystem Purpose, Boundaries, and Dependencies\n\nServer-Side Apply (SSA) is a declarative mutation model in which a client submits its *intent* — a\npartial description of an object containing only the fields it cares about — and the API server\nperforms the merge, tracks field ownership via `metadata.managedFields`, and surfaces ownership\nconflicts. This differs from a `PUT` (full replacement) or a strategic-merge-patch (client-computed\ndelta) in a fundamental way: the server is the authoritative arbiter of which manager owns which\nfield, and it maintains that record across reconciliation loops.\n\nThe subsystem spans two distinct layers:\n\n| Layer | Location | Role |\n|---|---|---|\n| Client | `staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/` | Typed, partial-object builders used to construct apply requests |\n| Server | `staging/src/k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/util/managedfields/` | Field-ownership tracking and structured merge |\n| HTTP entry | `staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/endpoints/handlers/patch.go` | Routes PATCH requests with `Content-Type: application/apply-patch+yaml` into the SSA path |\n| Code generation | `staging/src/k8s.io/code-generator/cmd/applyconfiguration-gen/` | Generates the client-side builder types |\n\n**Upstream dependencies:**\n\n- `sigs.k8s.io/structured-merge-diff/v6` — the external library that performs the actual structural\n  merge and conflict detection. The Kubernetes code calls `merge.Updater.Apply()` and\n  `merge.Updater.Update()` from this library.\n- `k8s.io/kube-openapi` — provides `schemaconv.ToSchemaFromOpenAPI` which converts the OpenAPI\n  v2/v3 spec into the SMD schema format used for typed merges.\n- `k8s.io/gengo/v2` — the code generation framework used by `applyconfiguration-gen`.\n\n**Downstream consumers:**\n\n- Every typed client in `staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/kubernetes/typed/` exposes an `Apply()`\n  method that accepts an applyConfiguration type and calls the SSA HTTP endpoint.\n- Controllers in `pkg/controller/` (e.g., `pkg/controller/clusterroleaggregation/`) use SSA for\n  all write operations to avoid the read-modify-update race condition.\n- `kubectl apply --server-side` translates user-supplied YAML into an SSA request.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Key Components and Responsibility Splits\n\n### 2.1 The applyconfigurations Package\n\n`staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/`\n\nEvery Kubernetes API type has a parallel \"apply configuration\" type. The core property of these\ntypes is that **all fields are pointers**, so a field that is never set serialises as absent (rather\nthan as its zero value). This is the problem the package solves:\n\n```go\n// Regular struct: spec.maxReplicas marshals as 0 if omitted — wrong for an apply request.\nhpa := v1.HorizontalPodAutoscaler{Spec: v1.HorizontalPodAutoscalerSpec{MinReplicas: ptr(0)}}\n\n// Apply configuration: omitted fields are simply absent from the YAML.\nhpaAC := v1ac.HorizontalPodAutoscaler(name, ns).\n    WithSpec(v1ac.HorizontalPodAutoscalerSpec().WithMinReplicas(0))\n```\n\nThis distinction is not cosmetic. If `maxReplicas` is included in the serialised patch, the server\nwill interpret it as this manager claiming ownership of the `maxReplicas` field and will set it,\npossibly overriding another manager's intent. The pointer-for-all-fields design prevents accidental\nfield claiming.\n\nEach apply configuration type is named `<Type>ApplyConfiguration`. The package also exposes:\n\n- `With<FieldName>()` builder methods that accept values (not pointers), isolating callers from the\n  pointer mechanics.\n- Constructor functions like `DeploymentApplyConfiguration()` for nested structs, and\n  `Deployment(name, namespace)` for root types that pre-populate `kind` and `apiVersion`.\n- `Extract<Type>(obj, fieldManager)` functions that populate an applyConfiguration from the\n  managed-fields owned by a given manager (see §4 for the extract workflow).\n\nThe top-level `utils.go` file (`staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/utils.go`) is\nalso generated: it provides `ForKind(gvk)` which returns a blank applyConfiguration interface{}\nindexed by GVK, useful when working dynamically.\n\n### 2.2 applyconfiguration-gen\n\n`staging/src/k8s.io/code-generator/cmd/applyconfiguration-gen/`\n\nThe code generator that produces the applyconfigurations package. It operates on the API Go types\nand an OpenAPI schema file.\n\nKey files:\n\n- `generators/targets.go` — entry point; calls `packageTypesForInputs`, builds the `refGraph` and\n  `typeModels`, then invokes per-type generators.\n- `generators/applyconfiguration.go` — generates the struct type and all `With*` methods for a\n  single type. Also generates the `Extract<Type>Into` function when an OpenAPI type is available.\n- `generators/refgraph.go` — builds the reference graph of which API types reference which, to\n  determine which types need an apply configuration generated.\n- `args/args.go` — defines `ExternalApplyConfigurations`, a map that tells the generator to reuse\n  existing apply configurations for types it does not control (e.g., `metav1.Condition` maps to\n  `k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/meta/v1`).\n\nA type requires an apply configuration if and only if it is a struct type with JSON-tagged fields\n(`requiresApplyConfiguration` in `refgraph.go`). Top-level API types (those with ObjectMeta) get\nboth an `Apply()` client method and an `Extract()` function; embedded types get only the struct and\n`With*` builders.\n\n### 2.3 The managedfields Package\n\n`staging/src/k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/util/managedfields/`\n\nThis is the server-side engine. The public surface is small:\n\n```go\n// fieldmanager.go\nfunc NewDefaultFieldManager(...) (*FieldManager, error)\nfunc NewDefaultCRDFieldManager(...) (*FieldManager, error)\n```\n\n`FieldManager` (re-exported from `internal.FieldManager`) has two methods:\n\n```go\nfunc (f *FieldManager) Update(liveObj, newObj runtime.Object, manager string) (runtime.Object, error)\nfunc (f *FieldManager) Apply(liveObj, appliedObj runtime.Object, manager string, force bool) (runtime.Object, error)\n```\n\n- `Update` handles regular PUT/PATCH operations: it does not merge; it simply recomputes which\n  fields changed and updates the manager's ownership record.\n- `Apply` handles the SSA path: it merges the applied configuration into the live object.\n\nOther public files:\n\n- `extract.go` — `ExtractInto()`: reconstructs an applyConfiguration from the managed fields owned\n  by a given manager.\n- `scalehandler.go` — `ScaleHandler`: translates managed field paths between a resource and its\n  `scale` subresource (because `spec.replicas` lives at a different path in a `Scale` object vs. in\n  a `Deployment`).\n- `typeconverter.go` — `TypeConverter` interface and `NewTypeConverter()`: converts\n  `runtime.Object` to `typed.TypedValue` using the OpenAPI-derived schema.\n- `gvkparser.go` — parses GVK from a YAML file; used in tests.\n\n#### 2.3.1 The Manager Decorator Stack\n\n`NewDefaultFieldManager` composes a layered stack of `Manager` interface implementations. The order\nfrom outermost to innermost is:\n\n```\nVersionCheckManager                  (versioncheck.go)\n  └─ LastAppliedUpdater              (lastappliedupdater.go)\n       └─ LastAppliedManager         (lastappliedmanager.go)\n            └─ ProbabilisticSkipNonAppliedManager  (skipnonapplied.go)\n                 └─ CapManagersManager             (capmanagers.go)\n                      └─ BuildManagerInfoManager   (buildmanagerinfo.go)\n                           └─ ManagedFieldsUpdater (managedfieldsupdater.go)\n                                └─ StripMetaManager (stripmeta.go)\n                                     └─ structuredMergeManager  (structuredmerge.go)\n```\n\nEach layer is responsible for exactly one concern:\n\n| Layer | File | Responsibility |\n|---|---|---|\n| `VersionCheckManager` | `internal/versioncheck.go` | Rejects apply requests whose GVK does not match the resource GVK |\n| `LastAppliedUpdater` | `internal/lastappliedupdater.go` | When the field manager is `\"kubectl\"` and the live object has a `kubectl.kubernetes.io/last-applied-configuration` annotation, syncs that annotation with the new object body after a successful apply |\n| `LastAppliedManager` | `internal/lastappliedmanager.go` | On the first SSA from kubectl, migrates the annotation-based `last-applied-configuration` into managed fields |\n| `ProbabilisticSkipNonAppliedManager` | `internal/skipnonapplied.go` | For objects with no managed fields, skips tracking on updates (lazy bootstrapping). On create, tracks managed fields with probability `DefaultTrackOnCreateProbability` (currently 1.0). On the first apply to an untracked object, bootstraps by running an `Update` with the live object under a synthetic `\"before-first-apply\"` manager before the actual apply |\n| `CapManagersManager` | `internal/capmanagers.go` | Limits update-manager entries to `DefaultMaxUpdateManagers` (10) by merging the oldest entries into versioned buckets named `\"ancient-changes\"` |\n| `BuildManagerInfoManager` | `internal/buildmanagerinfo.go` | Serialises the manager name, operation type, API version, and subresource into a canonical JSON key used as the manager identifier in the internal `fieldpath.ManagedFields` map |\n| `ManagedFieldsUpdater` | `internal/managedfieldsupdater.go` | Sets the timestamp (`metav1.Time`) on the manager's entry when fields change. Uses a temporary `\"current-operation\"` manager key during `Update` to detect whether any fields actually changed |\n| `StripMetaManager` | `internal/stripmeta.go` | After the merge, removes server-managed fields (`apiVersion`, `kind`, `metadata.name`, `metadata.namespace`, `metadata.managedFields`, `metadata.resourceVersion`, etc.) from the manager's field set so they are never tracked |\n| `structuredMergeManager` | `internal/structuredmerge.go` | Converts objects to SMD `typed.TypedValue`, calls the SMD library's `merge.Updater.Update()` or `.Apply()`, then converts the result back |\n\nThe `Manager` interface itself is defined in `internal/manager.go`:\n\n```go\ntype Manager interface {\n    Update(liveObj, newObj runtime.Object, managed Managed, manager string) (runtime.Object, Managed, error)\n    Apply(liveObj, appliedObj runtime.Object, managed Managed, fieldManager string, force bool) (runtime.Object, Managed, error)\n}\n```\n\nNote that the inner signature carries `Managed` (the decoded field ownership map) separately from\nthe object, so the object stored in etcd never carries the managed fields during internal\nprocessing. `EncodeObjectManagedFields` / `RemoveObjectManagedFields` are used at the\n`FieldManager` boundary to translate between the wire format and the internal representation.\n\n### 2.4 structuredMergeManager and the SMD Library\n\n`staging/src/k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/util/managedfields/internal/structuredmerge.go`\n\nThis is the only layer that calls the external SMD library. It holds a `merge.Updater` configured\nwith:\n\n- A `Converter` — either `newVersionConverter` (for built-in types) or\n  `newCRDVersionConverter` (for CRDs) — which the SMD library calls to convert objects between API\n  versions during merge.\n- An `IgnoreFilter` — a `fieldpath.Filter` map (`resetFields`) specifying which fields to ignore\n  per API version (used to strip server-side defaults from managed-field tracking, e.g., for\n  `status` on create).\n\nFor an apply operation, `structuredMergeManager.Apply()`:\n1. Validates that the patch object's GVK matches `f.groupVersion`.\n2. Rejects patch objects that contain `metadata.managedFields` (clients must not submit managed fields).\n3. Converts both the patch object and the live object to `typed.TypedValue`.\n4. Calls `f.updater.Apply(liveObjTyped, patchObjTyped, apiVersion, managed.Fields(), manager, force)`.\n5. Converts the result back: first to a versioned object, runs the defaulter, then converts to the hub (internal) version.\n\nThe SMD library returns `merge.Conflicts` if `force=false` and another manager owns a field in the\npatch. The `FieldManager.Apply()` method in `internal/fieldmanager.go` catches this and calls\n`NewConflictError()` to produce an HTTP 409 with structured `StatusCause` entries.\n\n### 2.5 TypeConverter\n\n`staging/src/k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/util/managedfields/internal/typeconverter.go`\n\n`TypeConverter` bridges Kubernetes objects and the SMD library:\n\n```go\ntype TypeConverter interface {\n    ObjectToTyped(runtime.Object, ...typed.ValidationOptions) (*typed.TypedValue, error)\n    TypedToObject(*typed.TypedValue) (runtime.Object, error)\n}\n```\n\n`NewTypeConverter(openapiSpec, preserveUnknownFields)` builds a `typeConverter` by:\n1. Calling `schemaconv.ToSchemaFromOpenAPI` to produce the SMD schema.\n2. Iterating over the OpenAPI spec to extract `x-kubernetes-group-version-kind` extensions and\n   building a `map[schema.GroupVersionKind]*typed.ParseableType`.\n\n`ObjectToTyped` looks up the parser by GVK. If the GVK is not in the map, it returns a\n`NoCorrespondingTypeError`, which causes the merge to fall back to treating the object atomically.\n\nFor CRDs, `NewDeducedTypeConverter()` returns a `deducedTypeConverter` that calls\n`typed.DeducedParseableType.FromUnstructured()`; all lists are treated as atomic because there is\nno schema information to determine merge keys.\n\n---\n\n## 3. End-to-End Request Flow\n\n### 3.1 HTTP Entry Point\n\nAll PATCH requests arrive at `PatchResource()` in\n`staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/endpoints/handlers/patch.go`:\n\n```go\nfunc PatchResource(r rest.Patcher, scope *RequestScope, admit admission.Interface, patchTypes []string) http.HandlerFunc\n```\n\nThe handler reads the `Content-Type` header and routes to different `patchMechanism`\nimplementations:\n\n| Content-Type | Mechanism |\n|---|---|\n| `application/json-patch+json` | `jsonPatcher` |\n| `application/merge-patch+json` | `jsonPatcher` |\n| `application/strategic-merge-patch+json` | `patcher` (strategic merge) |\n| `application/apply-patch+yaml` | `applyPatcher` |\n| `application/apply-patch+cbor` | `applyPatcher` (behind feature gate) |\n\nFor SSA, the `applyPatcher` struct is used:\n\n```go\n// patch.go:488\ntype applyPatcher struct {\n    patch               []byte\n    options             *metav1.PatchOptions\n    creater             runtime.ObjectCreater\n    kind                schema.GroupVersionKind\n    fieldManager        *managedfields.FieldManager\n    userAgent           string\n    validationDirective string\n    unmarshalFn         func(data []byte, v interface{}) error\n    unmarshalStrictFn   func(data []byte, v interface{}) error\n}\n```\n\n### 3.2 Apply Sequence\n\nThe `patcher.patchResource()` method calls the storage layer's `GuaranteedUpdate`, which\nin turn calls `p.applyPatch()` in a retry loop:\n\n```go\n// patch.go:578-620 (applyPatch)\nfunc (p *patcher) applyPatch(ctx context.Context, _, currentObject runtime.Object) (...) {\n    if !currentObjectHasUID {\n        // Object does not exist yet: create and apply\n        objToUpdate, patchErr = p.mechanism.createNewObject(ctx)\n    } else {\n        // Object exists: apply patch to current state\n        objToUpdate, patchErr = p.mechanism.applyPatchToCurrentObject(ctx, currentObject)\n    }\n    ...\n}\n```\n\n`applyPatcher.applyPatchToCurrentObject()` (patch.go:500):\n1. Deserialises the YAML/CBOR body into an `*unstructured.Unstructured`.\n2. Calls `p.fieldManager.Apply(obj, patchObj, p.options.FieldManager, force)`.\n3. If `fieldValidation=Strict`, additionally validates the YAML with the strict decoder.\n\n`applyPatcher.createNewObject()` (patch.go:532):\n1. Creates an empty object with `p.creater.New(p.kind)`.\n2. Calls `applyPatchToCurrentObject` on the empty object — the SMD merge against an empty live\n   object is effectively a create.\n\n### 3.3 Inside FieldManager.Apply\n\n`internal/fieldmanager.go:183`:\n```go\nfunc (f *FieldManager) Apply(liveObj, appliedObj runtime.Object, manager string, force bool) (object runtime.Object, err error) {\n    managed, err := DecodeManagedFields(accessor.GetManagedFields())  // decode from wire format\n    object, managed, err = f.fieldManager.Apply(liveObj, appliedObj, managed, manager, force)\n    if err != nil {\n        if conflicts, ok := err.(merge.Conflicts); ok {\n            return nil, NewConflictError(conflicts)  // convert to HTTP 409\n        }\n        return nil, err\n    }\n    if err = EncodeObjectManagedFields(object, managed); err != nil { ... }\n    return object, nil\n}\n```\n\nThe call traverses the decorator stack (see §2.3.1), bottom to top:\n\n1. `structuredMergeManager.Apply()` performs the SMD merge, producing a new merged object and\n   updated `fieldpath.ManagedFields`.\n2. `StripMetaManager` removes system fields from the manager's field set.\n3. `ManagedFieldsUpdater` sets the timestamp on the manager's entry.\n4. `BuildManagerInfoManager` has already serialised the manager identifier before passing down.\n5. `CapManagersManager` (no-op for Apply, applies only on Update).\n6. `ProbabilisticSkipNonAppliedManager` handles the bootstrap case.\n7. `LastAppliedManager` migrates annotation-based tracking if needed.\n8. `LastAppliedUpdater` syncs the kubectl annotation.\n9. `VersionCheckManager` validated the GVK before delegating.\n\n### 3.4 Inside structuredMergeManager.Apply\n\n`internal/structuredmerge.go:121`:\n\n```go\nfunc (f *structuredMergeManager) Apply(liveObj, patchObj runtime.Object, managed Managed, manager string, force bool) (runtime.Object, Managed, error) {\n    // 1. Version check: patchObj GVK must match f.groupVersion\n    // 2. Reject patch objects with managedFields\n    liveObjVersioned, _ := f.toVersioned(liveObj)\n    patchObjTyped, _ := f.typeConverter.ObjectToTyped(patchObj)        // no AllowDuplicates: patch must be clean\n    liveObjTyped, _ := f.typeConverter.ObjectToTyped(liveObjVersioned, typed.AllowDuplicates)\n    apiVersion := fieldpath.APIVersion(f.groupVersion.String())\n\n    // 3. The SMD merge\n    newObjTyped, managedFields, err := f.updater.Apply(liveObjTyped, patchObjTyped, apiVersion, managed.Fields(), manager, force)\n\n    // 4. Convert back: typed → unstructured → default → hub version\n    newObj, _ := f.typeConverter.TypedToObject(newObjTyped)\n    newObjVersioned, _ := f.toVersioned(newObj)\n    f.objectDefaulter.Default(newObjVersioned)\n    newObjUnversioned, _ := f.toUnversioned(newObjVersioned)\n    return newObjUnversioned, NewManaged(managedFields, managed.Times()), nil\n}\n```\n\nThe defaulter runs on the merged result so that server-filled defaults (e.g., `imagePullPolicy`)\nare present before the result is stored.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Conflict Handling\n\n### 4.1 What Constitutes a Conflict\n\nA conflict occurs when the applied configuration includes a field that is already owned by a\n**different** manager in the live object's managed fields. The SMD library's `merge.Updater.Apply`\nreturns a `merge.Conflicts` value (a slice of `{Manager, Path}` pairs) in this case.\n\n### 4.2 Conflict Error Representation\n\n`internal/conflict.go:33`:\n\n```go\nfunc NewConflictError(conflicts merge.Conflicts) *errors.StatusError {\n    causes := []metav1.StatusCause{}\n    for _, conflict := range conflicts {\n        causes = append(causes, metav1.StatusCause{\n            Type:    metav1.CauseTypeFieldManagerConflict,\n            Message: fmt.Sprintf(\"conflict with %v\", printManager(conflict.Manager)),\n            Field:   conflict.Path.String(),\n        })\n    }\n    return errors.NewApplyConflict(causes, getConflictMessage(conflicts))\n}\n```\n\nThe HTTP response is a 409 Conflict. Each `StatusCause` identifies the field path and the manager\nthat currently owns it.\n\n### 4.3 Resolution Patterns\n\n| Pattern | Mechanism | Trade-off |\n|---|---|---|\n| Force takeover | `Force: true` in `ApplyOptions` | Unconditionally reassigns ownership; appropriate for controllers that fully own their fields |\n| Remove the field | Omit the field from the next apply config | Relinquishes ownership; the field will be managed by whoever claimed it last |\n| Use a different fieldManager name | Change `FieldManager` string | Allows co-ownership under distinct manager identities |\n| Read-extract-modify-apply | Use `ExtractInto` to base the apply config on existing ownership | Avoids accidental ownership claims; suitable for legacy controller migration |\n\n### 4.4 Force-Apply Behaviour\n\nWhen `force=true`, the SMD library reassigns field ownership from the conflicting managers to the\ncaller without returning an error. The previous owner's field set is narrowed to exclude the\ncontested fields. If the previous owner's field set becomes empty, its entry is removed from\n`metadata.managedFields`.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Workflow Patterns\n\n### 5.1 Full-Reconciliation (Mechanism 1)\n\nSuitable for new controllers that own a well-defined set of fields:\n\n```go\n// pkg/controller/clusterroleaggregation/clusterroleaggregation_controller.go:143\nclusterRoleApply := rbacv1ac.ClusterRole(name).\n    WithRules(toApplyPolicyRules(newPolicyRules)...)\n\nopts := metav1.ApplyOptions{FieldManager: \"clusterrole-aggregation-controller\", Force: true}\n_, err := c.clusterRoleClient.ClusterRoles().Apply(ctx, clusterRoleApply, opts)\n```\n\nThe controller reconstructs the full apply configuration on every reconciliation and sets\n`Force: true`. This guarantees idempotency and handles the case where a human or other controller\nhas modified a field the controller owns.\n\n### 5.2 Extract-Modify-Apply (Mechanism 2)\n\nSuitable for controllers with conditional code paths where not all fields are always set:\n\n```go\n// staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/doc.go:130\nfieldMgr := \"my-field-manager\"\ndeployment, _ := deploymentClient.Get(ctx, \"example-deployment\", metav1.GetOptions{})\n\n// Extract only the fields currently owned by this manager\ndeploymentAC, _ := appsv1ac.ExtractDeployment(deployment, fieldMgr)\n\n// Modify in place\ndeploymentAC.Spec.Template.Spec.WithContainers(\n    corev1ac.Container().WithName(\"app\").WithImage(\"nginx:1.14.2\"),\n)\n\n// Apply; the resulting patch contains only the manager's owned fields plus modifications\napplied, _ := deploymentClient.Apply(ctx, deploymentAC, metav1.ApplyOptions{FieldManager: fieldMgr})\n```\n\n`ExtractDeployment` calls `managedfields.ExtractInto`:\n\n```go\n// managedfields/extract.go:53\nfunc ExtractInto(object runtime.Object, objectType typed.ParseableType, fieldManager string,\n                 applyConfiguration interface{}, subresource string) error {\n    typedObj, _ := toTyped(object, objectType)\n    fieldsEntry, ok := findManagedFields(accessor, fieldManager, subresource)\n    if !ok { return nil }  // no fields owned: leave applyConfiguration empty\n    fieldset := &fieldpath.Set{}\n    fieldset.FromJSON(bytes.NewReader(fieldsEntry.FieldsV1.Raw))\n    u := typedObj.ExtractItems(fieldset.Leaves()).AsValue().Unstructured()\n    runtime.DefaultUnstructuredConverter.FromUnstructured(u.(map[string]interface{}), applyConfiguration)\n    return nil\n}\n```\n\nKey details:\n- `ExtractItems(fieldset.Leaves())` extracts only the *leaf* fields (not intermediate nodes). This\n  produces the minimal representation that the manager is entitled to reclaim.\n- The function must be called on the **root** resource object, not a subresource, because only root\n  objects carry their own managed fields.\n- If the manager has no managed fields yet (first invocation), the function returns without error\n  and `applyConfiguration` is left at its zero value.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Managed Fields Encoding\n\n### 6.1 Wire Format\n\n`metadata.managedFields` is a `[]metav1.ManagedFieldsEntry`. Each entry has:\n\n```go\ntype ManagedFieldsEntry struct {\n    Manager     string\n    Operation   ManagedFieldsOperationType  // \"Apply\" or \"Update\"\n    APIVersion  string\n    Time        *Time\n    FieldsType  string    // always \"FieldsV1\"\n    FieldsV1    *FieldsV1  // JSON-encoded fieldpath.Set\n    Subresource string\n}\n```\n\n### 6.2 Internal Format\n\nInternally, managed fields are a `fieldpath.ManagedFields` (a `map[string]fieldpath.VersionedSet`),\nwhere the key is a JSON-serialised manager identifier (produced by `BuildManagerIdentifier`).\n\nThe identifier includes manager name, operation, API version (for updates only), and subresource.\nFor **apply** operations, the APIVersion is excluded so the same applier retains a stable identity\nacross version upgrades:\n\n```go\n// internal/managedfields.go:148\nif encodedManager.Operation == metav1.ManagedFieldsOperationApply {\n    encodedManagerCopy.APIVersion = \"\"\n}\n```\n\nFor **update** operations, the API version is included, which means a controller that upgrades the\nAPI version it uses generates a new manager entry (old entry is merged into the bucket of the\nsame version or lost if the CapManagersManager threshold is reached).\n\n### 6.3 Decode/Encode Cycle\n\n`DecodeManagedFields()` reads from the wire format; `EncodeObjectManagedFields()` writes back.\nThe encode path sorts entries (by operation, time, manager name, API version, subresource) so that\ndiffs between stored objects are deterministic.\n\n---\n\n## 7. Failure Modes, Limits, and Design Tradeoffs\n\n### 7.1 Failure Modes\n\n| Failure | Behaviour |\n|---|---|\n| Patch YAML fails to deserialise | HTTP 400 Bad Request |\n| Patch GVK does not match resource GVK | HTTP 400 (caught by `VersionCheckManager`) |\n| Patch includes `metadata.managedFields` | HTTP 400 (caught by `structuredMergeManager`) |\n| Field conflict without `force=true` | HTTP 409 with `StatusCause` per conflicting field |\n| `TypeConverter` has no schema for the GVK | `NoCorrespondingTypeError`; falls back to atomic merge semantics |\n| `EncodeObjectManagedFields` fails | HTTP 500; managed fields are cleared from the returned object |\n| `UpdateNoErrors` path (used for regular updates) | On error, managed fields are cleared silently; logged at most once per second |\n\n### 7.2 Limits\n\n- **Max update managers:** 10 (`DefaultMaxUpdateManagers`). Exceeded entries are merged into\n  per-API-version `\"ancient-changes\"` buckets by `CapManagersManager`. This is a lossy operation:\n  once merged, individual manager history is gone.\n- **No limit on apply managers:** Apply managers are not capped, because each represents an active\n  intent owner. Objects with many SSA controllers accumulate proportionally.\n- **`metadata.managedFields` size:** Unbounded in practice; large numbers of apply managers or\n  field sets on large objects can inflate object size noticeably.\n\n### 7.3 Design Tradeoffs\n\n**Pointer-for-all-fields** in applyconfigurations is the right design for expressing partial\nintent, but it makes the generated types incompatible with the normal API types. Controllers must\nmaintain parallel codepaths — one using the normal type for reads, one using the apply\nconfiguration for writes. The extract-modify-apply pattern bridges this.\n\n**CRD merge semantics:** CRDs use a deduced type converter that treats all lists as atomic, meaning\nSSA on CRD list fields replaces the entire list rather than merging by key. To get true list-merge\nsemantics, a CRD must provide an OpenAPI schema with `x-kubernetes-list-type: map` and\n`x-kubernetes-list-map-keys` annotations. Without a schema, the \"structured merge\" is structurally\natomic.\n\n**Probabilistic tracking on create:** `DefaultTrackOnCreateProbability` is 1.0, meaning all new\nobjects are tracked from creation. Setting it lower reduces overhead for objects never touched by\nSSA, but makes the first apply more expensive (the bootstrap path runs an `Update` to record\npre-existing fields).\n\n**kubectl annotation compatibility:** The `LastAppliedUpdater` syncs the\n`kubectl.kubernetes.io/last-applied-configuration` annotation only when the field manager is\n`\"kubectl\"`. Operators using a custom field manager name bypass this sync. This is intentional\n(opt-out by name) but means tools that depend on the annotation (some GitOps systems) may see stale\nvalues if a custom-named SSA controller is used.\n\n---\n\n## 8. Extension Points and Customisation Hooks\n\n### 8.1 Custom Field Managers\n\nThe `fieldManager` string in `ApplyOptions` is the primary extension point. Each unique value\ncreates an independent ownership domain. Conventions:\n\n- Controllers should use a stable, unique name (e.g., `\"clusterrole-aggregation-controller\"`).\n- kubectl uses `\"kubectl\"` for SSA and `\"kubectl-client-side-apply\"` for the annotation-based\n  approach (defined as `FieldManagerClientSideApply` and `fieldManagerServerSideApply` in\n  `staging/src/k8s.io/kubectl/pkg/cmd/apply/apply.go`).\n- Using `\"kubectl\"` as a custom controller's field manager triggers the annotation-sync logic in\n  `LastAppliedUpdater`, which is unlikely to be desired.\n\n### 8.2 resetFields (IgnoreFilter)\n\n`NewDefaultFieldManager` and `NewDefaultCRDFieldManager` accept a\n`map[fieldpath.APIVersion]fieldpath.Filter` parameter called `resetFields`. This filter is passed\nto the SMD library's `merge.Updater.IgnoreFilter` and tells the merger to strip certain fields from\nfield ownership tracking — for example, `status` on create (since status is server-filled).\n\nResources that want to prevent certain fields from being owned by any manager wire this up when\nthey install the FieldManager. There is no hook for controllers to provide custom `resetFields`;\nthis is configured by the API server at resource registration time.\n\n### 8.3 External Apply Configurations\n\nThe `applyconfiguration-gen` tool accepts `--external-applyconfigurations` (defined in\n`args/args.go`) to reuse existing generated apply configurations for types from other packages. The\ndefault includes:\n\n```go\n{Package: \"k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/apis/meta/v1\", Name: \"Condition\"}: \"k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/meta/v1\"\n```\n\nProjects that generate their own apply configurations for CRD types use this mechanism to point the\ngenerator at the right package for shared types.\n\n### 8.4 CEL-Based Apply Configuration in Mutating Admission Policies\n\nA newer extension point: `MutatingAdmissionPolicy` (alpha) supports a `PatchTypeApplyConfiguration`\nmutation type. The admission plugin compiles a CEL expression that returns an apply configuration\nobject, then calls `ApplyStructuredMergeDiff()` in\n`staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/admission/plugin/policy/mutating/patch/smd.go` to apply it.\nThis uses the same `managedfields` machinery but invokes it inside the admission chain rather than\nat the storage layer.\n\n### 8.5 ScaleHandler for Subresource Mapping\n\n`managedfields.NewScaleHandler` is the hook used by resources that expose a `scale` subresource.\nIt translates field paths between the main resource schema and the `autoscaling/v1.Scale` schema.\nResources that need this wire it in their REST handler. It is not extensible from outside the\nKubernetes codebase without forking.\n\n---\n\n## 9. Operational Implications\n\n### 9.1 Performance\n\n**Apply is more expensive than Update.** An apply involves:\n1. Deserialising the entire live object from storage.\n2. Converting both the live and patch objects to `typed.TypedValue` (involves OpenAPI schema\n   lookup and typed traversal).\n3. Running the SMD merge algorithm (graph traversal over the field tree).\n4. Re-defaulting the merged object.\n5. Serialising the managed fields and storing the result.\n\nRegular updates only need step 5 (plus the storage round-trip). For high-frequency writes on large\nobjects (e.g., node status updates), the SSA path has measurable overhead; status subresource\nupdates remain as regular `Update` operations for this reason.\n\n**TypeConverter is a hot path.** The `typeConverter.parser` map is built once at startup from the\nOpenAPI spec and is read-only during serving. No lock is needed for reads. For CRDs with a schema,\nthe CRD handler refreshes the type converter on schema changes.\n\n### 9.2 managedFields Bloat\n\n`metadata.managedFields` grows with the number of distinct managers and API versions. Contributors\nto field bloat:\n\n- Many SSA controllers on the same object.\n- API version upgrades: update managers carry the version in their identity, so upgrading from\n  `apps/v1beta1` to `apps/v1` adds new entries. Old entries are eventually merged into\n  `\"ancient-changes\"` if the 10-entry cap is reached.\n- Large field sets on objects with many fields (e.g., Nodes).\n\nThe `CapManagersManager` provides a hard limit for update managers but not for apply managers.\nOperators should avoid giving each reconciliation loop a unique field manager name (e.g., do not\ninclude a pod name in the field manager string).\n\n### 9.3 Reliability and Conflict Avoidance\n\nSSA makes multi-controller collaboration explicit and reliable, but requires coordination:\n\n- **Controllers must use `Force: true`** if they are the authoritative owner of a field.\n  Not using `Force` means a stale field set (from a previous manager) can block the controller's\n  apply and cause continuous reconciliation errors.\n- **Removing fields from an apply config** implicitly relinquishes ownership. If a controller\n  conditionally omits a field, and another manager claims it, the original controller can no longer\n  set that field without a conflict or force.\n- **Admission webhooks** that mutate objects during apply are opaque to managed fields: mutations\n  performed by webhooks are attributed to the original applier's field manager, which can cause\n  surprising ownership. The `MutatingAdmissionPolicy` CEL approach is the structured alternative.\n\n### 9.4 Maintainability\n\n**Generated code must be regenerated** whenever an API type changes. The `Makefile` target\n`generate` runs `applyconfiguration-gen`. Failing to regenerate causes compile errors in the typed\nclient, since the `Apply()` method signature references the apply configuration type.\n\nThe separation between the `applyconfigurations` package (generated, all-pointer structs) and the\nAPI types (generated, mixed value/pointer structs) means there are two type systems in parallel.\nThis is a known maintenance cost accepted in exchange for correctness.\n\n---\n\n## 10. File and Directory Map\n\n| Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|\n| `staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/` | Generated client-side apply configuration types, one subdirectory per API group/version |\n| `staging/src/k8s.io/client-go/applyconfigurations/doc.go` | Package-level documentation;
